L35: ## 1 Executive Summary
L36: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L37: Mirdametinib is a small molecule inhibitor of mitogen-activated protein kinases 1 and 2 (MEK1/2). MEK1/2 proteins are upstream regulators of the extracellular signal-related kinase (ERK) pathway. Activation of this signaling pathway leads to cell proliferation and is involved in various types of cancer (Yaeger and Corcoran, 2019).
L38: Neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) is an autosomal dominant disorder with variable expressivity caused by germline mutations in the NF1 gene. Plexiform neurofibromas (PNs) are benign tumors of the peripheral nerve sheath which occur in 40-50% of patients with NF1. PNs may be associated with significant morbidity including pain, disfigurement, neurologic deficits, and functional impairment; and mortality from malignant transformation to malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumors (MPNST) and compression of vital structures (Ly and Blakeley, 2019). In a mouse model of neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1), oral dosing of mirdametinib inhibited ERK phosphorylation and reduced neurofibroma tumor volume and proliferation.
L39: In this new drug application (NDA), the Applicant proposed the following indication for mirdametinib:
L40: - Adult and pediatric patients 2 years of age and older with neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) who have symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas.
L41: The review team recommends approval for adult and pediatric patients 2 years of age and older with neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) who have symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas (PN) not amenable to complete resection.
L42: The recommended dosage of mirdametinib is 2 mg/m2 orally twice daily with or without food for the first 21 days of each 28-day cycle, until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.
L43: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L44: The review team recommends regular approval of mirdametinib for the treatment of adult and pediatric patients 2 years of age and older with neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) who have symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas not amenable to complete surgical resection. The recommendation for approval is primarily supported by overall response rate (ORR) and duration of response (DOR) results from Study MEK-NF-201 (which will be referred to as the ReNeu study in the review). In this study, mirdametinib demonstrated a robust and clinically meaningful effect on ORR that is durable and associated with improvements in disease-related morbidities. These factors represent direct clinical benefit to patients and support regular
L45: approval. The safety profile for mirdametinib is consistent with other MEK inhibitors and is acceptable in the context of a serious and life-threatening disease.
L46: The ReNeu study is a multicenter, single-arm trial investigating mirdametinib in adult and pediatric patients with NF1 and progressive or symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas not amenable to complete surgical resection. Eligible patients were at least 2 years of age with PN causing significant morbidity, and a target PN that was evaluable by volumetric analysis. Patients were enrolled by age group into pediatric and adult cohorts. Treatment consisted of mirdametinib at a dosage of 2 mg/m2 orally twice daily for the first 21 days of each 28-day cycle. The study included a treatment phase lasting 24 cycles and an optional long-term follow-up phase. The primary endpoint to demonstrate the efficacy of mirdametinib for this population was confirmed ORR, defined as PN decrease \ge 20\% compared to baseline in consecutive scans within 2-6 months, using independent central review of MRI volumetric analysis. DOR was a key secondary endpoint.
L47: The primary efficacy population consisted of 114 patients, including 56 pediatric and 58 adult patients. The confirmed BICR-assessed ORR in pediatric patients was 52% (95% CI: 38, 65); the median DOR was not reached. A total of 90% of responders had a DOR of \ge 12 months and 48% of responders had a DOR of \ge 24 months. The confirmed ORR as assessed by BICR in adult patients was 41% (95% CI: 29, 55); the median DOR was not reached. A total of 88% of responders had a DOR of \ge 12 months and 50% of responders had a DOR of \ge 24 months. Supportive data for the application were derived from the observed ORR in 19 patients treated with mirdametinib on Study NF-106 [32% (95% CI: 13, 57), a single arm, multicenter trial of adolescent and adult patients with an unresectable PN with progression within the past year.
L48: In addition to radiographic tumor shrinkage, the ReNeu study evaluated mirdametinib effects on PN-related functional impairment, symptoms and disfigurement using various clinical outcome assessment (COA) measures as well as longitudinal photographic evaluations. Despite the limitations of the single-arm trial design, small sample size, heterogeneity of associated morbidities and interpretation of the COA measures, a general trend toward an association between a reduction in tumor volume and durable improvement in clinical outcomes was observed.
L49: Given the benign, albeit morbid, nature of NF1-PN, the Applicant submitted reports from the literature indicating that a key characteristic of NF1 PN is the uncommon occurrence of spontaneous regression, supporting the conclusion that the observed tumor responses in ReNeu are due to the effect of the drug. This supportive information includes reports from the placebo control arm of the National Cancer Institute (NCI) Study 01-C-0222, a randomized, placebo-controlled trial of tipifarnib in pediatric patients with NF1 and progressive PNs; and NCI Study 08-C-0079, a natural history study of children, adolescents, and adults with NF1.
L50: ## NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
## Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
L51: FDA considers ORR, supported by DOR, to be an appropriate endpoint for regular approval for this rare patient population given that their clinical meaningfulness is associated with improved clinical outcome assessment data from the ReNeu study. Furthermore, while rare instances of spontaneous PN volume decrease have been observed, the magnitude of these reductions are not comparable to the tumor regression observed with mirdametinib treatment. There are no approved therapies for adults with NF1-PN. Selumetinib is a MEK inhibitor that is approved for pediatric patients with symptomatic, inoperable NF1-PN; however, the approval of mirdametinib addresses an unmet need for pediatric patients with NF1-PN who are unable to swallow capsules.
L52: The observed ORR and DOR with mirdametinib are robust and clinically meaningful and the tumor shrinkage results are associated with a positive trend in improved clinical outcomes in patients with symptomatic NF1-PN not amenable to surgical resection, and provide substantial evidence of effectiveness. The safety findings support a favorable benefit:risk assessment, and the review team recommends regular approval.
L53: ## NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
## Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
L54: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L55: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L56: Neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) is an autosomal dominant disorder with variable expressivity caused by germline mutations in the NF1 gene. Plexiform neurofibromas (PNs) are benign tumors of the peripheral nerve sheath which occur in 40-50% of patients with NF1. More than 50% of NF1-PNs are detected in early childhood, and the fastest growth rate is typically during the first decade of life. PNs may be associated with significant morbidity including pain, disfigurement, neurologic deficits, and functional impairment (Ly and Blakely, 2019; Dombi et al, 2007). PNs may also lead to mortality through compression of vital structures, e.g., airway compression and hemothorax (Prada et al, 2012). Malignant transformation to malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumors (MPNST) can occur in 8-16% of patients with NF1-PN.
L57: Treatment typically includes surgical resection; however, resection is often not feasible due tumor effect on adjacent tissue, nerves, and vasculature. The only FDA-approved therapy for NF1-PN is the MEK inhibitor selumetinib, for pediatric patients 2 years of age and older with NF1 who have symptomatic, inoperable PN. The reported ORR for selumetinib is 44% (95% CI: 30, 59) according to Response Evaluation in Neurofibromatosis and Schwannomatosis (REINS) criteria (i.e. PN decrease \ge20% compared to baseline in consecutive scans within 3-6 months) by independent central review (Koselugo USPI). Selumetinib is currently only available in a capsule formulation and is therefore not accessible to pediatric patients who cannot swallow capsules. There are currently no approved systemic therapies for adult patients with NF1-PN.
L58: This NDA for mirdametinib is primarily supported by results from the ReNeu study, a multicenter, single-arm trial investigating mirdametinib in adult and pediatric patients with NF1 and progressive or symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas not amenable to complete surgical resection. The primary efficacy population included 114 patients, including 56 pediatric and 58 adult patients with NF1-PN. All patients in the study had measurable disease and received mirdametinib at a dosage of 2 mg/m2 orally twice daily for the first 21 days of each 28-day cycle, for up to 24 cycles in the treatment phase.
L59: The primary endpoint to demonstrate the efficacy of mirdametinib for this population was confirmed ORR defined as PN decrease \ge20% compared to baseline in consecutive scans within 2-6 months, using independent central review of MRI volumetric analysis. Because the study calendar required end-of-study imaging at 3 months (+/- 10 days) from the second to last imaging timepoint, these modified criteria were used to capture responses that occurred late in the study with less than 3 months between consecutive scans. DOR was a key secondary endpoint. The confirmed BICR-assessed ORR in the primary efficacy population of pediatric patients was 52% (95% CI: 38, 65); the median DOR was not
L60: ## NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
## Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
L61: reached. A total of 90% of responders had a DOR of \ge 12 months and 48% of responders had a DOR of \ge 24 months. The confirmed ORR as assessed by BICR in the primary efficacy population of adult patients was 41% (95% CI: 29, 55); the median DOR was not reached. A total of 88% of responders had a DOR of \ge 12 months and 50% of responders had a DOR of \ge 24 months.
L62: The primary safety population for this NDA comprises a pooled population of 133 patients (58 pediatric and 75 adult) treated with mirdametinib at the recommended dosage on the ReNeu study or Study NF-106. The most common adverse events in the ReNeu study in adult patients (&gt;40\%) were rash, diarrhea, nausea and musculoskeletal pain, and the most common adverse events in pediatric patients (&gt;40\%) were rash, diarrhea, and musculoskeletal pain. Serious adverse reactions in the ReNeu study occurred in 17% of adult patients; serious adverse reactions occurring in \ge 1\% of adult patients were COVID-19 (3.4%), nephrolithiasis (3.4%), acute kidney injury (1.7%), abdominal pain (1.7%), ischemic colitis (1.7%), urinary tract infection (1.7%), retinal vein occlusion (1.7%), scoliosis (1.7%), squamous cell carcinoma of skin (1.7%), cerebrovascular accident (1.7%) and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (1.7%). Serious adverse reactions in the ReNeu study occurred in 14% of pediatric patients; serious adverse reactions occurring in \ge 1\% of patients included viral gastrointestinal infection (3.6%), diplopia (1.8%), musculoskeletal pain (1.8%), seizure (1.8%), fall (1.8%), femoral neck fracture (1.8%), dehydration (1.8%), and hypertension (1.8%). One fatal adverse reaction occurred in an adult patient (1.7%) due to COVID-19. No additional safety signals were identified in the pooled population. The safety profile of mirdametinib is consistent with that of other MEK inhibitors, and important safety concerns include ocular toxicity, left ventricular dysfunction, dermatologic adverse reactions, and embryo-fetal toxicity. These safety concerns will be addressed in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI. The safety profile for mirdametinib is considered acceptable when assessed in the context of a serious and life-threatening disease and with appropriate monitoring as described in the product label. Postmarketing requirements (PMRs) will be issued for an integrated safety analysis of data to further characterize the long-term incidence, severity, and outcomes of known serious risks including ocular toxicity, left ventricular dysfunction, and dermatologic adverse reactions as well as to further characterize the potential serious risk of long-term adverse effects of mirdametinib on growth and development in pediatric patients. PMRs are described in further detail in Section 13.
L63: The results of the ReNeu study demonstrate substantial evidence of effectiveness for the treatment of adult and pediatric patients 2 years of age or older with NF1 who have symptomatic PN not amenable to complete surgical resection. The magnitude of the observed treatment effect on ORR and DOR is clinically meaningful and is supported by a positive trend in COA data. Given the rarity of NF1-PN and the associated morbidity in this patient population, a randomized trial is not considered feasible.
L64: ## NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
## Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
L65: Based on the results of the ReNeu study, the totality of information submitted to this NDA, and the favorable benefit:risk profile for this population with a serious and life-threatening disease, the review team recommends regular approval for mirdametinib for the following indication: the treatment of adult and pediatric patients 2 years of age and older with neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) who have symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas not amenable to complete surgical resection.
L66: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	NF1 is a progressive multi-system disease caused by loss of NF1 function and characterized by progressive cutaneous, neurological, skeletal, and neoplastic manifestations early in life and which can be associated with severe morbidity.
PNs are benign nerve sheath tumors that occur in 40-50% of patients with NF1. PNs may develop along nerves throughout the body and may be located around the orbit, face, upper and lower limbs, back, thorax, abdomen, neck brachial plexus and/or lumbosacral plexus, which result in clinical symptoms such as disfigurement, motor dysfunction, pain, respiratory impairment, visual dysfunction, and neurological dysfunction.
Malignant transformation to malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumors (MPNST) can occur in 8-16% of patients with NF1-PN.
NF1 is a rare, progressive, and highly morbid condition that can lead to fatal outcomes depending upon the location of the tumor. It is a serious and life-threatening disease.
Current Treatment Options	Selumetinib is approved for pediatric patients 2 years of age and older with symptomatic, inoperable NF1-PN, and demonstrated an ORR of 44% as assessed by independent central review. Selumetinib is available in a capsule formulation.
There are no available systemic therapies for adult patients
There is an unmet medical need for adult patients with progressive or symptomatic NF1-PN, and for pediatric patients who are unable to swallow capsules.

L81: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L82: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L83: ## The Applicant's Position:
L84: Neurofibromatosis is classified into three major clinically and genetically distinct forms: neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1), neurofibromatosis type 2, and schwannomatosis. NF1, also known as von Recklinghausen disease, is the most common form of neurofibromatosis. NF1 is a rare, autosomal dominant genetic disorder with an annual birth incidence of approximately 1 in 2500 individuals (Children's Tumor Foundation 2023; Lee 2023). Approximately one-half of cases are inherited with the remainder being the result of de novo mutations in the NF1 tumor suppressor gene, located at chromosome 17q11.2 (Evans 2010; Ledbetter 1989; Feldkamp 1998).
L85: Neurofibromin, the protein product encoded by the NF1 gene, belongs to a family of guanosine triphosphate hydrolase (GTPase)-activating proteins that down-regulate the cellular proto-oncogene p21-ras (21 kD rat sarcoma viral oncogene homologs) (Martin 1990; Weiss 1999; Gutmann 2013). Ras activates numerous signaling pathways, including the mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) pathways. Ras has been implicated in the control of cell growth and differentiation, and the ability of neurofibromin to down-regulate p21-ras suggests that the loss of neurofibromin may lead to uncontrolled cellular growth or tumor formation.
L86: Mutations in the NF1 gene result in loss of production or reduced function of neurofibromin, causing the wide spectrum of clinical findings, including NF1-associated tumors (Shen 1996). Penetrance, or the likelihood that the individual carrying the mutation will manifest the disorder, is complete. The loss of neurofibromin in malignancy supports the notion that neurofibromin is a tumor-suppressor protein (Bergoug 2020).
L87: NF1 is characterized by diverse, progressive cutaneous, neurological, skeletal, and neoplastic manifestations. The manifestation of the NF1 clinical diagnostic criteria typically appear in the order of café-au-lait macules, axillary and/or inguinal freckling, Lisch nodules (iris hamartomas), and finally neurofibromas (DeBella 2000). Most importantly, patients with NF1 develop both benign and malignant tumors at increased frequency throughout life (Gutmann 1997; Seminog 2013). The diagnostic criteria for NF1 were defined at a National Institute of Health (NIH) consensus development conference in 1987 (NIH Consensus Development Conference Statement 1987) and updated in 2021. In most cases, the diagnosis of NF1 is made on clinical grounds, requiring 2 or more clinical features to be present from the defined list of diagnostic criteria for NF1 presented below:
L88: - Six or more café-au-lait-macules*
L89: ## NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
## Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
L90: - &gt; 5mm in pre-pubertal children
- &gt; 15mm in post-pubertal individuals
- Freckling in axilla (armpit) or groin*
- Two or more neurofibroma tumors of any type, or 1 PN
- Two or more Lisch nodules (iris hamartomas) or two or more choroidal abnormalities – defined as bright, patchy nodules imaged by optical coherence tomography /near-infrared reflectance imaging
- Optic pathway gliomaA distinctive osseous lesion such as a sphenoid dysplasia, anterolateral bowing of tibia, or pseudarthrosis of a long bone
- A heterozygous pathogenic *NF1* variant with a variant allele fraction of 50% in apparently normal tissue such as white blood cells
- A child of a parent who meets the diagnostic criteria specified above merits a diagnosis of NF1 if one or more of the criteria above are present
L91: *At least one of the two pigmentary findings (café-au-lait macules or freckling) should be bilateral. (Legius 2021)
L92: Neurofibromas are benign peripheral nerve sheath tumors that are comprised of a mixture of Schwann cells, fibroblasts, perineural cells, and mast cells (Tucker 2011). When a neurofibroma extends longitudinally along a nerve and involves multiple fascicles, it is classified as a plexiform neurofibroma (PN). PNs grow most rapidly during the first decade of life and while the growth rate is highly variable between patients, the growth rate of PN in younger children is generally much greater compared to older children or adults (Dombi 2007, Nguyen 2012, Tucker 2009). A Natural History study of NF1 conducted by the National Cancer Institute (NCI) Pediatric Oncology Branch demonstrated that, although spontaneous PN shrinkage occurred in a small number of PNs in older adolescent and adult patients, the shrinkage is very slow, taking place over many years, and no patient had spontaneous PN shrinkage \ge20% within a year (Dombi 2007, Gross 2018). As such, PNs rarely regress spontaneously, and in many patients their growth is relentless. PNs occur in 30%-50% of patients with NF1 and represent a major cause of morbidity and disfigurement in individuals with NF1, and when symptomatic, are associated with increased mortality (Prada 2012; Miller 2019). As tumor growth progresses, PNs produce dysfunction, pain, and cosmetic disfigurement and can compress vital structures such as the airway or spinal cord. Once a clinical morbidity develops in patients with PN that are growing, it is extremely unlikely for the morbidity to resolve spontaneously (Gross 2018). Chronic pain in NF1 can be associated with PN, as well as subcutaneous neurofibroma and bony deformations (scoliosis and pseudoarthrosis), and as such, can occur anywhere in the body or multiple sites at the same time (Bellampalli 2019). Furthermore, PNs have the potential to undergo malignant transformation into malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumors (MPNSTs), which occur in an estimated 8%-16% of patients with NF1 (Evans 2002; Hirbe 2014). MPNSTs are aggressive tumors that can metastasize, with a 5-year survival rate of 20%-30% (Imura 2021).
L93: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L94: FDA agrees with the Applicant's description of the condition. FDA also notes that during the NF1 Natural History study conducted at the NCI, spontaneous gradual tumor volume was confirmed in 10/113 (8.8%) of PNs, with a median decrease from maximum volume of 19% and a median decrease per year of 3.6% (Akshintala et al, 2020).
L95: On June 13, 2019, FDA hosted a listening session for patients with NF, which included those affected by PN, and their caregivers. During this session, patients described the symptoms, daily burden, and impact of living with NF. Common themes in their narratives included the physical burden of chronic pain, multiple surgeries, motor dysfunction, side effects of chemotherapy, and the social and emotional toll of disfigurement. For example, one 19-year-old male with NF1 and multiple PNs, including a tumor which compressed his spinal cord, described undergoing over twenty surgeries and receiving chemotherapy for 17 years to control PN growth. He also described bullying by classmates that required him to change schools.
L96: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L97: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L98: The approach to treatment of the various tumors associated with NF1 depends upon the type of tumor, its effect on adjacent tissues, and related complications. PNs usually involve multiple nerve fascicles, with serpiginous growth and significant vascularity. These lesions can represent a significant challenge in surgical treatment and pain management, especially with progressive growth along the spinal column that may result in compression of the spinal cord (Gold 2013).
L99: The management of these complex lesions varies. Patients were more likely to benefit from surgery when the indications were airway compression or disfigurement (Canavese 2011). However, up to 44% of tumors progress after the first surgery, most commonly in patients younger than 10 years of age, with head and neck tumors that could not be completely resected (Needle 1997). Surgical resection is often limited to debulking of a specific area of a large lesion, for example, when a component is impinging on the spinal cord or airway, or a large soft tissue component is removed to improve cosmesis. Investigational studies of various tyrosine kinase inhibitors, mTOR inhibitors, a farnesyl transferase inhibitor, and fibroblast inhibitors have not demonstrated meaningful or durable objective responses in patients with progressive PN (Jakacki 2017, Robertson 2012, Weiss 2014, Widemann 2014, Widemann 2014b). One treatment option, selumetinib (Koselugo®), an oral selective MAPK inhibitor, is currently approved in the United States (US) for the treatment of pediatric patients ≥ 2 years with NF1 who have symptomatic, inoperable PNs (Koselugo 2024). However, Koselugo does not have an age-appropriate formulation and cannot be administered to patients who have difficulty swallowing a capsule whole.
L100: ## NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
## Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
L101: The pivotal study in support of the selumetinib approval was a Phase 2 study (SPRINT) that enrolled 50 participants aged 3 to &lt; 18 years with inoperable, morbid NF1 PNs. The primary endpoint was objective response rate (ORR) per the Response Evaluation in Neurofibromatosis &amp; Schwannomatosis (REINS) per a single reviewer and resulted in an ORR of 66% (95% CI: 51, 79); using the same dataset, an independent centralized review of the final tumor response best overall response (BOR) per the REINS criteria resulted in an ORR of 44% (95% CI: 30, 59) (Koselugo 2024). Common side effects (\ge40%) were vomiting, rash, abdominal pain, diarrhea, nausea, dry skin, fatigue, musculoskeletal pain, pyrexia, acneiform rash, stomatitis, headache, paronychia, and pruritus. Serious adverse reactions occurred in 24% of participants with the most common AEs including anemia, hypoxia, and diarrhea. Permanent discontinuation due to an adverse reaction occurred in 12% of participants. Dose interruptions and dose reductions due to adverse reactions occurred in 80% and 24% of participants, respectively (Koselugo 2024).
L102: There is no curative treatment for NF1 and there are no approved pharmacological treatments for adult patients \ge18 years of age with NF1 PN, which represents approximately 81% of the total patient population (Ejerskov 2023). Mirdametinib was studied in both adults and pediatric patients with NF1 PN aged 2 years and above. Mirdametinib not only provides as a treatment option in pediatric patients and addresses the medical need in children with difficulty swallowing given its dispersible tablet formulation, it also addresses the unmet need in the adult population with NF1 PN.
L103: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L104: FDA agrees with the Applicant's position, and that there is an unmet medical need for adults with symptomatic, inoperable NF1 PN as well as pediatric patients who are unable to swallow capsules.

L135: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L136: FDA agrees with the Applicant's timeline, with the following additional comments regarding the discussion at selected meetings:
L137: ## • **Pre-IND meeting August 29, 2018**
L138: - FDA stated that the Applicant would need to conduct a full battery of GLP-compliant reproductive toxicology studies and carcinogenicity studies and recommended that the Applicant submit carcinogenicity protocols for special protocol assessment review by FDA prior to initiation of studies (subsequently reviewed by FDA on June 25, 2024). The Applicant confirmed that an embryo-fetal study was conducted in the rabbit, however FDA noted that the data from the EFD study were not submitted. FDA recommended that the Applicant submit the results of the study and ask for FDA feedback regarding whether pre and post-natal development and pivotal EFD studies are necessary.
- FDA stated that the single-arm MEK-NF-201 study, with a primary endpoint of radiographic response, might support an application seeking marketing approval, provided that the natural history of the disease has been adequately established. FDA also stated that the study should demonstrate a clinically meaningful magnitude of response rate, that response rates are sufficiently durable, and unequivocal evidence of an important treatment effect that is supported by data showing an improvement in physical function or an improvement in specific symptoms related to progressive PN disease that correlates with radiographic tumor shrinkage.
L139: ## • **Type C meeting Jan 29, 2019**

- FDA provided comments regarding the submitted SAP for the analysis of PROs/COAs. The Applicant acknowledged this advice.

L154: keeping the 1 mg strength is acceptable based on the dosing regimen. NDA 219389, submitted simultaneously, contains relevant data to support the capsule formulation and cross references all other modules in mirdametinib tablet NDA 219379 for supporting data. The Applicant's requests to designate mirdametinib tablets for oral suspension and capsules as a BCS Class 1 drug substance/drug product were granted. For routine quality control testing of mirdametinib tablets, the Applicant's proposal to use disintegration testing (instead of dissolution testing) is acceptable. L159: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L160: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L161: Mirdametinib is an orally bioavailable small molecule non-ATP competitive inhibitor of mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK)/extracellular signal regulated kinases 1 and 2 (MEK1/2). MEK1/2 proteins are upstream regulators of the extracellular signal-related kinase (ERK) pathway. Both MEK and ERK are critical components of the RAS-regulated RAF-MEK-ERK pathway, which is often activated in different types of cancers. The proposed indication for mirdametinib is the treatment of adult and pediatric patients with neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1)-related tumors. Neurofibromin (encoded by NF1) contains a RAS-GTPase activating domain. Mutations in the NF1 gene result in loss of production or reduced function of neurofibromin, causing hyperactive Ras signaling, that results in a wide spectrum of clinical findings, including benign or malignant NF1-associated tumors.
L162: The established pharmacological class of mirdametinib is kinase inhibitor. In biochemical assays, mirdametinib inhibited the kinase activity of MEK1 and MEK2 with inhibitory constant (Ki) values of 1.1 nM and 0.79 nM, respectively. In a cell-free signaling cascade assay, mirdametinib inhibition of MEK reduced the level of phosphorylated ERK1 (pERK1) with a Ki value of 0.9 nM. Mirdametinib and its pharmacologically active unique human metabolite, M22, reduced the levels of pERK in cells in vitro, but M22 demonstrated considerably lower ability to reduce the levels of pERK (&gt;91-times lower). In addition, the carboxylic acid metabolite of mirdametinib, PD-0315209, demonstrated inhibitory activity against MEK1 phosphorylation in a cell-based assay (IC_{50}=1.3 \muM) but was less active compared to mirdametinib (IC_{50}=0.59 nM). The Applicant further demonstrated the antitumor activity of mirdametinib in a transgenic mouse model of NF1 neurofibroma (Nf1fl/fl;Dhh-Cre). The Applicant cites literature [Jessen (2013), Jousma (2015)] to support the claim that in this model, deletion of *Nf1* gene in Schwann cells exhibits similar neurofibroma growth and histology to human neurofibromas. In this model, spontaneous neurofibromas are detected by magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) at approximately 4 months of age. Daily treatment of animals with mirdametinib for two months (from 7 to 9 months of age) resulted in reduced tumor growth compared to untreated animals. Protein lysates of tumor tissue harvested from mice treated with mirdametinib showed reduced levels of pERK, a downstream target of MEK signaling. The results of the pharmacology studies support the proposed mechanism of action of mirdametinib and proof-of-concept for the proposed indication.
L163: In a panel of 104 kinases, mirdametinib at a concentration of 1 \muM resulted in &gt;70% inhibition of 4 kinases (MEK1, B-Raf, B-RafV600E, and C-Raf). The effect of mirdametinib on MEK2 was not evaluated in this study. In a separate panel of 87 receptors, enzymes, ion channels, and transporters, mirdametinib at a concentration of 10 \muM resulted in &gt;50% inhibition of
L164: ## Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
L165: peroxisome proliferator activated receptor gamma (PPAR\gamma) and the L-type calcium channel (phenylalkylamine). Although the Applicant did not conduct follow up assays to determine IC_{50} values, the 1 \muM concentration of mirdametinib tested in secondary pharmacology assays is approximately 800-fold higher than the free C_{max}. Additionally, the inhibition noted on B-Raf and C-Raf kinases may be a consequence of MEK inhibition and not a direct effect on targets considering that the Applicant used a linked kinase cascade assay format, containing other kinases of the MAPK signaling cascade in the reaction.
L166: In safety pharmacology studies, mirdametinib did not affect cardiovascular parameters in monkeys at single oral doses \le10 mg/kg. Mirdametinib or its metabolite, PD-0315209, did not affect hERG current in an in vitro assay at clinically relevant concentrations (&lt;10% inhibition at 10 mM). Mirdametinib did not affect CNS parameters in rats after single IV doses \le150 mg/kg, but a single oral dose of 100 mg/kg resulted in reduced locomotor activity 24 hours post-dose. In an in vitro assay, mirdametinib reduced action potential amplitude, but not resting membrane potential, at a concentration of 100 mM in isolated canine Purkinje fibers. Increased respiratory rate and decreased tidal volume occurred in rats after single oral doses of 30 or 100 mg/kg mirdametinib but no effect was noted at 10 mg/kg.
L167: Mirdametinib was highly bound in plasma from humans (\ge99.7%), mice, rats, and dogs. Following a single oral dose of 10 mg/kg, [^{14}C]-mirdametinib-derived radioactivity distributed widely to rat tissues, with greatest uptake in the liver, followed by kidney, blood, adrenal, lung, salivary gland, heart, and pancreas. Radioactivity was not retained in pigmented tissues, suggesting no affinity for melanin. The highest concentrations of [^{14}C]-mirdametinib-derived radioactivity were noted 4 hours after oral administration and radioactivity was undetectable by 48 hours post-dose. Following oral administration to rats, the primary excretion route was the feces. PD-0315209 (a carboxylic acid metabolite) and M22 (a glucuronide conjugate; a unique human metabolite) have been identified as major human metabolites (&gt;10%) of mirdametinib. Both metabolites are pharmacologically active, though activity is significantly lower than mirdametinib. PD-0315209 was detected in rats and dogs in the 3-month repeat-dose toxicology studies with exposure up to 20-times higher in animals than clinical exposure. Because M22 is a Phase 2 glucuronide conjugate and Phase 2 conjugation reactions generally render a compound more water soluble and pharmacologically inactive, and consistent with FDA Guidance for Industry Safety Testing of Drug Metabolites, further evaluation is not warranted.
L168: Mirdametinib was shown to absorb light in the 290 to 700 nm range, indicating probable phototoxicity.
L169: The Applicant is developing a tablet for dissolution formulation (NDA 219379) for pediatrics and a capsule formulation (NDA 219389) for adults. Based on discussion with the clinical pharmacology review team, the exposure to mirdametinib in pediatric and adults, using either
L170: formulation, is not significantly different. Therefore, animal to human exposure multiples were calculated using human pharmacokinetic (PK) parameters of C_{max} of 189 ng/mL and AUC_{last} of 445 ng\cdoth/mL.
L171: The toxicity profile of mirdametinib administered orally, the intended route of administration, was evaluated in GLP-compliant general toxicology studies of up to 3-months duration in rats and dogs. Though mortalities did not occur in rats at doses \le 1 mg/kg in the 1- or 3-month studies or dogs at doses \le 0.3 mg/kg in the 1-month study, two male and two female dogs at the high dose level of 0.4 mg/kg in the 3-month study were euthanized in moribund condition on Days 37 to 50. Common clinical signs in early death dogs included diarrhea, emesis, and soft stools that correlated with decreased food consumption and body weight loss. Other findings in early death dogs suggestive of a systemic inflammatory response included neutrophilic infiltrates/inflammation in the tongue, stomach, and/or esophagus as well as lymphoid depletion in multiple lymphoid organs and myeloid cell hyperplasia in the bone marrow; increases in circulating leukocytes and neutrophils were noted in individual animals. Due to severe clinical signs in the high dose group, dosing in the surviving male and female dogs at 0.4 mg/kg was stopped early on Days 51 and Day 57, respectively. Clinical signs of emesis, abnormal stools were also noted at doses \ge 0.1 mg/kg in dogs in the 3-month study.
L172: Signs of skin toxicity were noted in rats at doses \ge 0.1 mg/kg and in dogs at 0.3 mg/kg in the 1- and 3-month studies and included clinical signs of skin sores that correlated with minimal to mild neutrophilic inflammation, crust, and/or ulceration of the epidermis. Consistent with nonclinical findings, dermatitis acneiform, non-acneiform rashes, and exfoliative rash were reported in patients, leading to a warning and precautions for skin toxicity in the label. In general, dermatologic adverse events are associated with other MEK inhibitors and are consistent with the role of MEK1/2 in the epidermis [Scholl (2007)].
L173: Hematologic changes in animals were noted at doses \ge 0.8-times the human exposure at the clinical dose of 2 mg/m^2 twice daily based on area under the curve (AUC). Decreases in red blood cell parameters, including red blood cells, hemoglobin, and hematocrit, occurred at doses \ge 0.1 mg/kg in rats and at \ge 0.05 mg/kg in dogs. Increases in circulating monocytes, neutrophils, and/or large unstained cells were noted in rats at \ge 0.3 mg/kg and dogs at \ge 0.05 mg/kg. These findings correlated with histologic signs in the lymph nodes in the 3-month studies in rats and dogs, including minimal to moderate lymphoid hyperplasia, lymphoid depletion, and increases in plasma cells, as well as hypocellularity in rats.
L174: Ocular findings in rats administered 1 mg/kg included minimal conjunctiva mineralization in the 1-month study and corneal mineralization in the 3-month study. Minimal atrophy and thinning of the cornea were also noted at doses \ge 0.3 mg/kg (\ge 2-times the clinical dose of 2 mg/m^2 twice daily based on AUC) in the 3-month study in rats. In the 3-month toxicology study in dogs, corneal opacity was noted during ophthalmic examinations in one male each at 0.05 mg/kg and
L175: ## Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
L176: 0.4 mg/kg and correlated with histologic signs of minimal neutrophilic inflammation and mild corneal atrophy in the 0.4 mg/kg male. Mineralization and atrophy of the cornea were added to section 13.2 of the label because they differed from the ocular toxicity listed (retinal vein occlusion, retinal pigment epithelium detachments and blurred vision) in the warning and precautions of the GOMEKLI label.
L177: In rats, minimal to mild mineralization was observed at doses \ge 0.3 mg/kg (\ge 2-times the human exposure at the clinical dose of 2 mg/m^2 twice daily based on AUC) in the 1-month and 3-month studies in multiple organs, including major arteries (pulmonary, renal, and/or mesenteric), kidney, liver, stomach, and GALT. Though signs of mineralization were not noted in dogs, increases in phosphorus occurred at all dose levels in rats (increased up to 37% compared to controls) and at \ge 0.05 mg/kg in dogs (increased up to 49% compared to controls) and improved after a drug-free period in the 1-month studies, suggesting reversibility. Minimal and transient increases in calcium were noted during the first week of dosing in the 1-month rat study, but no changes in calcium were noted in the 1-month study in rats or the 3-months studies in rats and dogs. Hyperphosphatemia and mineralization findings in animals were not added to section 13.2 of the label because the clinical relevance of these findings is unclear.
L178: Findings in the bone were noted in rats at 1 mg/kg in the 1-month study and at \ge 0.3 mg/kg in the 3-month study (\ge 2-times the human exposure at the clinical dose of 2 mg/m^2 twice daily based on AUC), including minimal to mild thickening of trabeculae and dysplasia of the epiphysis and compact bone. Bone toxicity is relevant given the pediatric population, with growing bones, included in this indication; therefore, bone findings in the repeat-dose studies in rats were added to section 8.4 of the label.
L179: There were slight but statistically significant increases in liver enzymes (ALT, AST, ALP) that correlated with minimal to mild mixed cell inflammation and/or hepatocyte vacuolation at doses \ge 0.1 mg/kg in the 1-month rat study and \ge 0.3 mg/kg in the 3-month rat study. Increases in liver enzymes were also noted in dogs at 0.3 mg/kg in the 1-month study and at 0.4 mg/kg in the 3-month study but lacked any histologic correlate. Additionally, increased liver enzymes in the 28-day study in mice correlated with dose-dependent increases in incidence of hepatocellular necrosis. Of note, liver had the highest drug uptake in tissue distribution studies. Considering the totality of nonclinical data, including increases in liver enzymes, marked liver vacuolation, and necrosis in other toxicology studies (3-month rat and 1-month mouse), which were also the cause of death in the 6-month carcinogenicity mouse study, mirdametinib induced hepatotoxicity at exposures \ge 2-times the human exposure at the clinical dose of 2 mg/m^2 based on AUC.
L180: Mirdametinib was negative in an in vitro bacterial reverse mutation test (Ames assay) and an in vitro human lymphocyte chromosomal aberration assay. The rat in vivo micronucleus assay showed equivocal results with mirdametinib. In a separate, follow-up assay, mirdametinib was
L181: negative for clastogenic potential in an in vivo chromosomal aberration assay in rats; however, this study did not rule out aneugenicity. Considering the totality of the data from both in vivo genotoxicity studies, the FDA has determined mirdametinib to be positive for aneugenicity. Though genetic toxicity of the PD-0315209 metabolite was not directly tested, it is expected that rats were exposed to PD-0315209 in the in vivo genetic toxicology tests as rats can generate PD-0315209. As discussed above, because M22 is a Phase 2 glucuronide conjugate, it is not expected to have an increased risk of carcinogenicity and further evaluation is not warranted.
L182: Plexiform neurofibromas with NF-1 are generally slow growing and depending on location are not immediately life-threatening but can be debilitating. Because of the potential for prolonged treatment and long-term survival in the proposed patient population, the Applicant conducted a carcinogenicity study and a dedicated fertility and early embryonic development study. In a 6-month carcinogenicity study in CByB6F1/Tg rasH2 hemizygous mice, the FDA concluded that no carcinogenic effect related to mirdametinib administration was observed at any dose level tested (up to 5 mg/kg/day). The Applicant is currently conducting a 2-year carcinogenicity study in rats and a post-marketing requirement (PMR) will be issued for the submission of the final study report.
L183: The effects of mirdametinib on fertility were assessed in a dedicated fertility study, where male rats were treated with mirdametinib at doses of 0.1, 0.3, or 1 mg/kg/day for 28 days before mating with untreated females to Gestational Day 1. In a separate cohort, female rats were treated with mirdametinib at doses of 0.1, 0.3, or 1 mg/kg/day for 14 days before mating with untreated males to Gestational Day 7. Though mirdametinib did not affect fertility in this study, findings from the repeat-dose toxicology studies indicate that mirdametinib may impair female fertility at exposures \ge 2-times the human exposure at the clinical dose of 2 mg/m^2 twice daily based on AUC. In the 3-month repeat-dose toxicology studies that did not include a recovery period, follicular cysts associated with decreased corpora lutea were noted in female rats at \ge 0.3 mg/kg and correlated with decreased ovarian organ weight. Based on findings from repeat-dose toxicology studies, FDA included an infertility section under 8.3 Females and Males of Reproductive Potential of the GOMEKLI label to inform infertility risk.
L184: Male reproductive organ findings noted at 1 mg/kg (\ge 8-times the human exposure at the clinical dose of 2 mg/m^2 twice daily based on AUC) in the 3-month study in rats included inflammation of the prostate in individual animals and marked hypoplasia of the spermatogenic epithelium in the testis and marked decreased content in the epididymis in one male. Additionally, degeneration of the testes was noted in a 28-day repeat-dose study in mice. Though the results of the dedicated fertility study were negative, male reproductive findings in rats may only be apparent when dosed at very high drug concentrations and/or after a longer duration of dosing due to differences in physiology compared to humans. Dose-dependent increases were noted in the incidence and severity of findings in male reproductive organs in
L185: ## NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
## Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
L186: the 3-month toxicology study in rats, despite low overall incidence. The potential for these findings to impair male fertility was discussed during the review. Considering all available data and literature supporting a role for MEK in spermatogenesis [Hasegawa (2013)], the possibility that male reproductive findings noted in animals were related to mirdametinib could not be ruled out. Therefore, male reproductive organ findings in rats were included in section 13.1 of the GOMEKLI label; however, the potential for impairment of male fertility was not included in section 8.3 of the GOMEKLI label given that the clinical impact of these findings is unclear.
L187: Based on the mechanism of action of mirdametinib and findings in animals, the pharmacology/toxicology team recommends a warning for embryo-fetal toxicity in the label for GOMEKLI. Embryo-fetal developmental (EFD) studies were conducted as dose range finding studies in rats and rabbits. Toxicokinetic evaluations were not included in the rat study; thus, for consistency, animal to human dose comparisons were made based on body surface area (BSA). Mirdametinib showed signs of maternal toxicity in rats at 5 mg/kg (30 mg/m2), including decreases in body weight gain and food consumption, as well as scabs, sores, and abrasions in the skin. Embryo-fetal toxicity at \ge 3 mg/kg (\ge 5-times the clinical dose of 2 mg/m2 twice daily based on BSA) included post-implantation loss and decreased fetal body weights. At 3 mg/kg (5-times the clinical dose of 2 mg/m2 twice daily based on BSA), multiple malformations, including shortening of limbs and absence or shortening of digits, were observed in one fetus and another fetus had a limb hyperflexion variation. In rabbits, maternal toxicity was evident at \ge 1 mg/kg (\ge 3-times the clinical dose of 2 mg/m2 twice daily based on BSA) and included decrease in body weight gain and moribundity leading to early sacrifice of all animals at \ge 3 mg/kg. Embryo-fetal toxicity in rabbits was observed at doses as low as 0.3 mg/kg (approximately equivalent to the clinical dose of 2 mg/m2 based on BSA) and included post-implantation loss and decreases in fetal body weight. Two animals had spontaneous abortions at the 1 mg/kg dose on Days 20 and 23 and total early fetal resorptions were reported at \ge 3 mg/kg. Based on the clear evidence of embryofetal lethality and teratogenicity in the dose range finding studies, the FDA agreed that no further reproductive toxicology testing (i.e., GLP embryo-fetal development and pre-and post-natal development studies) was warranted.
L188: FDA recommends advising females of reproductive potential to use effective contraception during treatment with GOMEKLI and for 6 weeks after the last dose and male patients with female partners of reproductive potential to use effective contraception during treatment with GOMEKLI for 3 months after the last dose. The recommendation is consistent with drugs that are teratogenic or embryo-fetal lethal and aneugenic and takes into consideration the half-life of 28 hours for mirdametinib. Because of the potential for serious adverse effects of GOMEKLI in a breastfed child, the review team recommends advising patients not to breastfeed during treatment with GOMEKLI and for 1 week after the last dose.
L189: ## Recommendation:
L190: From a pharmacology/toxicology perspective, the nonclinical data are adequate to support the
L191: approval of GOMEKLI for the proposed indication.
L192: ## 5.2. Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
L193: ## The Applicant's Position:
L194: There are no referenced NDAs, BLAs, or DMFs related to nonclinical pharmacology or toxicology for mirdametinib.
L195: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L196: FDA agrees.
L197: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L198: ## The Applicant's Position:
L199: Mirdametinib (PD-0325901) has been evaluated in numerous in vitro and in vivo nonclinical studies that were designed to characterize the pharmacology, safety pharmacology, pharmacokinetics (PK) and toxicology of the compound. The pharmacological properties of mirdametinib have been studied using enzyme and cell-based assays and evaluated in tumor xenograft models and in an NF1 mouse model. Biochemical assays have shown that mirdametinib is a selective, noncompetitive, allosteric inhibitor of mitogen-activated protein kinase kinase (MEK)-1 and MEK2 and thereby inhibits phosphorylation of the extracellular signal-regulated kinase (ERK)1 and ERK2, which in turn impairs tumor cell growth through the MAPK pathway.
L200: ## Primary Pharmacology
L201: As a potent MEK inhibitor, mirdametinib has an inhibition constant (K_i) for the activated forms of MEK1 and MEK2 of \le 1.1 nM. In a cell-based assay measuring inhibition of phosphorylated ERK1 as a measure of MEK inhibition of the Ras/Raf/MEK/ERK pathway, the K_i for mirdametinib was 0.90 nM.
L202: The specificity for various species was determined by comparing the potency of mirdametinib in cell-based assays using Colon-26 (mouse), MDCK (dog), and HCT-116 (human) cancer cell lines. The pharmacodynamic properties of mirdametinib are summarized in Table 2.
L203: **Table 2: Pharmacodynamic Properties of Mirdametinib Across Different Assay Types**

L205: Abbreviations: Colon-26: mouse colon adenocarcinoma cell line; HCT-116: human colorectal carcinoma cell line; HT-29: human colorectal adenocarcinoma cell line; IC50: 50% inhibitory concentration; IC90: 90% inhibitory concentration; MDCK: Madin-Darby canine kidney cell line; MEK: mitogen-activated protein kinase kinase; MiaPaCa-2: human pancreatic carcinoma cell line; pERK: phospho-extracellular signal-regulated kinase.
L206: a A cascade assay with Raf-1, MEK1, and ERK1 (DELFIA readout) was used to measure enzyme inhibition of MEK1.
L207: b Western blots were used to measure MEK inhibition in cultured cells exposed to the indicated compound for 1 hour.
L208: c Mice bearing Colon-26 tumors were treated with various oral doses of mirdametinib and the plasma concentrations along with decreases pERK levels in excised tumors were determined. From these values, the plasma concentrations required to inhibit ERK phosphorylation by 50% and 90% (IC50 and IC90) were calculated.
L209: d Tumor-bearing mice were treated orally with mirdametinib at 25 mg/kg; 24 hours postdose, pERK levels in excised tumors were measured by western blot analysis.
L210: e Mice bearing the C26 tumor were treated orally with 25 mg/kg/day mirdametinib for 14 days. A complete tumor response represents a decrease in tumor mass below the level of detection. Tumor growth delay is the difference in median days for the control and treated tumors to reach a specified evaluation size.
L211: Mirdametinib was shown to be highly efficacious in the *Nf1fl/fl;Dhh-Cre* mouse model of NF1 neurofibroma, in which *Nf1* gene deletion in Schwann cells exhibited similar neurofibroma growth and histology to human neurofibromas. These mice were treated with mirdametinib at
L212: ## NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
## Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
L213: 1.5, 5.0, or 10 mg/kg/day or vehicle via oral gavage once daily (QD) for 60 to 90 days, and small-animal magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) was used to evaluate tumor growth (Jessen 2013). Tumors measured by volumetric MRI analysis responded to a similar degree, with pronounced tumor reduction at all 3 dose levels (p &lt; 0.001) in &gt; 80% of mice (Figure 1), which correlated with associated reductions in biomarkers for cell proliferation (Ki67+) and blood vessels (MECA+ endothelial cells). Additionally, phosphor-extracellular signal-regulated kinase (pERK) was reduced in NF1 neurofibromas at both the high- (10 mg/kg) and low-dose groups (1.5 mg/kg): pERK disappeared by 30 minutes after dosing and remained low at 6 hours post dose; at 24 hours post dose, a sustained reduction in pERK was noted only in the low-dose group while pERK levels in the high-dose group had recovered.
L214: **Figure 1: Mirdametinib Inhibits Neurofibroma Growth in NF1 Mouse Model**
L215: Reference: Jessen 2013, Figure 2G.
L216: Abbreviations: PD: as abbreviation for mirdametinib, also known as PD-0325901, in X axis of this figure.
L217: Notes: Volumetric measurements of vehicle-treated or mirdametinib-treated Nf1^{fl/fl};Dhh-Cre NF1 mice indicate a decrease in neurofibroma volume treated with 1.5 mg/kg, 5 mg/kg, or 10 mg/kg PD 0325901 for 2 months. The y axis shows tumor volume in mm^3 quantified by measurements of MRI scans. Each bar represents the difference in tumor volume in an individual animal from Day 0 (7 months) to Day 60 (9 months). Mixed effects model analysis indicated statistical significance for each dose (p &lt; 0.001).
L218: The minimally effective dose (MED) of mirdametinib was evaluated in NF1 mice by treating mice with established tumors at the relatively low doses 0.5 and 1.5 mg/kg/day (Jousma 2015). In this study, 0.5 mg/kg dose group (human equivalent dose of approximately 2.5 mg twice daily [BID]) led to statistically significant neurofibroma shrinkage as compared to vehicle-treated mice. As shown in Figure 2, the extent of shrinkage seen in the 0.5 mg/kg mirdametinib group was comparable to the shrinkage of tumors from mice treated with mirdametinib at 1.5 mg/kg (dose was noted to provide a concentration similar to 8 mg BID in humans) (Jessen 2013). The low dose (0.5 mg/kg) of mirdametinib led to comparable reductions in tumor
L219: volume, blood vessel/area, and pERK levels with slightly higher frequencies of proliferating cells compared to 1.5 mg/kg dose (Figure 3).

L228: Mirdametinib is a potent MEK inhibitor, as demonstrated in a variety of species and nonclinical models. Evaluation in mouse models of NF1 that have similar histopathology and growth rates as human NF1 PNs demonstrated antitumor activity of mirdametinib treatment in NF1. Dose-ranging studies in NF1 mouse models also support the use of low doses of mirdametinib in achieving tumor reductions and demonstrate that NF1 neurofibromas have high sensitivity to MEK inhibition.
L229: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L230: In general, FDA agrees with the Applicant's conclusions. However, the Applicant uses terminology such as "selectively and efficiently inhibits," "superior," "clear," and "potent" to describe study results; such terms should be avoided as they are vague, subjective, and promotional. In addition to these findings, the FDA notes the following results:
L231: M22, a unique human metabolite of mirdametinib, demonstrated considerably lower ability to reduce ERK phosphorylation in HCT-116 cells (IC_{50} values of 362 nM to 835 nM). A carboxylic acid metabolite of mirdametinib, PD-0315209, also demonstrated activity as assessed by inhibition of MEK1 phosphorylation in a cell-based assay (IC_{50}=1.3 \muM); however, activity was lower compared to mirdametinib (IC_{50}=0.59 nM).
L232: In a mouse model of NF1 neurofibromas (*Nf1fl/fl;Dhh-Cre* mouse), intended to recapitulate
L233: neurofibroma growth and histology of human neurofibromas [Jessen (2013), Jousma (2015)], oral mirdametinib at doses as low as 0.5 mg/kg/day (1.5 \text{ mg}/\text{m}^2) reduced tumor growth in approximately 66% to 93% of neurofibroma-bearing animals.
L234: ## Secondary Pharmacology
L235: The Applicant's position:
L236: In a kinase selectivity assay with a select panel of 104 kinases (FR0950040003), 100 kinases were not inhibited after treatment with 1 \mu\text{M} mirdametinib. The other 4 kinases showed little to no kinase activity, including human MEK1 (21% activity), human B-Raf (0% activity), human B-Raf V599E (-1% activity), and human C-Raf (0% activity). Of note, the MEK1, B-Raf, B-Raf(V599E), and C-Raf kinases are linked assays exploiting kinase cascades in which the reaction mixtures contain components of the MAPK pathway, including MEK1. Therefore, B-Raf and C-Raf inhibition is consistent with the nature of the linked assay format for on-target inhibition by a MEK inhibitor.
L237: In addition, an in vitro safety screening assay (TW04-0016273) comprising 87 targets (receptors, enzymes, ion channels, and transporters) was conducted with 1 and 10 \mu\text{M} mirdametinib. At 10 \mu\text{M}, mirdametinib inhibited only 2 targets which included the L-type calcium channel (phenylalkylamine) inhibited by 54% and the peroxisome proliferator activated receptor gamma inhibited by 53%. Mirdametinib did not inhibit any targets at 1 \mu\text{M}.
L238: The screening assays covered a sufficient range of targets and did not demonstrate significant mirdametinib-mediated activity against proteins other than MEK1/2. The 1 \mu\text{M} concentration of mirdametinib tested in these assays is approximately 250-fold higher than the clinically observed unbound maximum observed concentration (C_{\text{max}}; 0.0039 \mu\text{M}) for mirdametinib in the MEK-NF-201 clinical study.
L239: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L240: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's assessment, except that based on a human plasma binding of 99.7% and a C_{\text{max}} of 189 ng/mL (392 nM), the free C_{\text{max}} is 1.2 nM. Thus, the 1 \mu\text{M} concentration of mirdametinib tested in secondary pharmacology assays is approximately 800-fold higher than the free C_{\text{max}}.
L241: ## Safety Pharmacology
L242: ## The Applicant's position:
L243: Safety pharmacology studies with mirdametinib assessed potential cardiac effects in good laboratory practice (GLP)-compliant in vitro human ether-a-go-go-related gene (hERG) and isolated Purkinje fiber assays, in GLP-compliant neurofunctional and respiratory studies in rats, and in a GLP-compliant cardiovascular study in telemetered monkeys, as outlined in Table 3.
L244: ## NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
L245: ## Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)

L252: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L253: FDA agrees with the results presented by the Applicant. The increased respiratory rate and decreased tidal volume noted in rats (Study# 745-03809) were up to 39% and 20%, respectively. Of note, the Applicant did not include females in the stand-alone safety pharmacology studies; however, effects on CNS, cardiovascular, and respiratory parameters were evaluated in the repeat-dose toxicology studies in rats and dogs that included both males and females. There were no effects on CNS parameters in rats at doses up to 1 mg/kg or dogs at doses up to 0.4 mg/kg after repeated dosing for up to 3 months. Mineralization of the arteries was noted in rats at 1 mg/kg in the 1-month and 3-month studies, but no cardiovascular effects were noted in dogs at doses up to 0.4 mg/kg. Though minimal to mild foamy macrophage infiltrates were noted in the lungs of rats at 1 mg/kg in the 3-month study, clinical signs of changes in respiratory parameters were not noted in rats or dogs.
L254: ## 5.4. ADME/PK
L255: ## The Applicant's Position:
L256: ## Data:
L257: ## **Absorption**
L258: *2-Week Oral Dose Range-Finding Study of PD-0325901 in Rats (745-03478)*
L259: *2-Week Oral Dose Range-Finding Study of PD-0325901 in Dogs (745-03537)*
L260: *Single Dose Pharmacokinetics and Oral Bioavailability of PD-0325901 in Fasted, Male Sprague-Dawley Rats Following Intravenous or Oral Administration of PD-0325901-0000 (764-04243)*
L261: *Single Dose Pharmacokinetics and Oral Bioavailability of PD-0325901 in Fasted, Male Beagle Dogs Following Intravenous or Oral Administration of PD-0325901-0000 (764-04241)*
L262: *Single Dose Pharmacokinetics and Oral Bioavailability of PD-0325901 in Fasted, Cynomolgus Monkeys Following Intravenous or Oral Administration of PD-0325901-0000 (764-04242)*
L263: In dose range finding studies in rats (745-03478) and dogs (745-03537), orally administered mirdametinib was rapidly absorbed and increased in a dose proportional manner. Time of the maximum concentration (Tmax) was reached by 1 hour in rats and ranged from 1 to 4 hours in dogs, with most animals reaching Tmax by 2 hours on Day 14. Plasma levels were similar between males and females at all doses. PK of mirdametinib was evaluated following oral and intravenous (IV) administration to male Sprague-Dawley rats (764-04243), male and female beagle dogs (764-04241), and male cynomolgus monkeys (764-04242). The absolute bioavailability was moderate to high (29.5%, 103%, and 43.7% in the rat, dog, and monkey, respectively).
L264: ## **Distribution**
L265: *In Vitro Evaluation of Protein Binding by Equilibrium Dialysis and Red Blood Cell Partitioning of [14C] PD-0325901 in Plasma and Red Blood Cells of Mouse, Rat, Dog, Monkey, Human and to Protein Solutions (HSA and AAG) (764-04271)*
L266: *Determination of Plasma, Brain, and Cerebrospinal Fluid (CSF) Concentrations of PD-0325901 and Metabolite PD-0325209 Following Intravenous Administration in Male Sprague-Dawley Rats (19SPRWP11R1)*
L267: *[14C] PD-0325901 Tissue Distribution in Male Rats Following a Single 10-mg/kg orally (PO) Dose or a Single 2-mg/kg IV Dose (764-04029)*
L268: *Binding to Human Plasma Proteins Human Blood to Plasma Partitioning (19SPRWP2R1)*
L269: Plasma protein binding for [14C] mirdametinib was high (\ge 98.7\%) in mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human and did not appear to be saturated over the concentration range studied of 5 to 100 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL} (764-04271). In the same study, binding to human serum albumin was determined to be \ge 99.7\% and did not appear to be saturated over the concentration range studied, whereas binding to \alpha 1-acid glycoprotein ranged from 17.2% to 54.3% suggesting the binding was saturable. Human plasma protein binding for the PD-0315209 metabolite was also high (99.975% at 1 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL}) (19SPRWP2R1). Whole blood to plasma ratios were generally similar across species and did not show meaningful variability with increasing drug concentration (764-04271).
L270: Mirdametinib is preferentially distributed to plasma. The distribution of mirdametinib and PD-0315209 into brain and CSF was evaluated in male rats following IV infusion of 1 \text{mg}/\text{kg} over 30 minutes (19SPRWP11R1). The half-life of mirdametinib in the rat brain was longer than in plasma and the brain to plasma ratio increased over time. PD-0315209 did not appear to enter the rat brain.
L271: A whole-body autoradiography study (764-04029) showed that radioactivity derived from [14C] mirdametinib is widely distributed throughout the body with [14C] mirdametinib equivalents in most tissues lower than in blood. For all tissues, the highest measured concentrations were observed within 4 hours and 0.5 hours postdose for the oral and IV routes, respectively. Drug equivalents were not retained in pigmented tissues. Distribution of [14C] mirdametinib equivalents in brain demonstrated distinct regional localization with highest levels in striatum, hippocampus, and olfactory bulb. [14C] mirdametinib equivalents in thoracic duct was approximately equal to blood through 24 hours after dosing, suggesting that drug equivalents may be recycled via intestinal lymphatics. Following IV administration of [14C] mirdametinib to male Long Evans rats, the distribution of [14C] mirdametinib equivalents was similar to that seen after oral dose administration. Evidence of recirculation was not as distinct. In both oral- and IV-dosed animals, high [14C] mirdametinib equivalents were present in bile ducts through the 24-hour collection
L272: time point, suggesting that biliary excretion is the main route of elimination. Renal excretion plays a minor role in elimination of mirdametinib as evidenced by low levels of [^{14}\text{C}] mirdametinib equivalents in the bladder.
L273: ## **Metabolism**
L274: *In Vitro Metabolism of [^{14}\text{C}] PD-0325901 by Rat, Dog, Monkey, and Human Liver Microsomes and Hepatocytes (76404904)*
L275: *Metabolic Stability in Human Liver Microsomes and Reaction Phenotyping in Recombinant Uridine diphosphate glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) Enzymes of Mirdametinib and Primary Metabolite (PD-0315209) (2009107_2009108)*
L276: *Reaction Phenotyping of PD-0325901 (Mirdametinib) in Human Recombinant Carboxylesterase (CES) Enzyme Supersomes (2102065)*
L277: *Urine, Bile, and Plasma Metabolic Profiling and Identification in Sprague-Dawley Rats Following a Single 3 mg/kg Oral Administration of [^{14}\text{C}] PD-0325901 (764-05091)*
L278: *Fecal and Plasma Metabolic Profiling and Identification in Beagle Dogs Following a Single 0.5 mg/kg Oral Administration of [^{14}\text{C}] PD-0325901 (764-05213)*
L279: In vitro metabolism of mirdametinib is consistent across nonclinical species and humans. All circulating metabolites observed in human liver microsomes were also present in rat, dog, and monkey liver microsomes and hepatocytes (764-04904). [^{14}\text{C}] mirdametinib undergoes both glucuronidation and oxidation of the parent compound. A carboxylic acid metabolite of mirdametinib (PD-0315209) was detected in microsomal incubations without the addition of NADPH as well as following preincubation with aminobenzotriazole, suggesting that this metabolite can be formed by non-CYP enzyme(s) with very little contribution by a single CYP isoform being identified in an in vitro study with recombinant enzymes. In vitro studies assessing the contribution of UGT to the metabolism of mirdametinib (showed that metabolism of mirdametinib was significant for UGT isoforms 1A6 (27.1%) and 2B7 (39.4%) (2009107_2009108). The percent contribution to metabolism of the primary metabolite was most significant for UGT isoform 1A6 (57.9%). A reaction phenotyping assay explored the contribution of CES to the metabolism of mirdametinib (2102065). Production of PD-0315209 was highest with CES1b followed by CES1c, indicating that these enzymes may contribute to the conversion of mirdametinib to the primary metabolite, PD-0315209.
L280: Mass balance and metabolite identification studies were conducted in both rat (764-05091) and dog (764-05213). Following oral administration of 3 mg/kg to rats, [^{14}\text{C}] mirdametinib underwent extensive metabolism via glucuronidation and oxidation. Combined parent drug and PD-0315209 accounted for approximately 80% to 90% of the total circulating radioactivity found in the rat. Beagle dogs (male and female) were
L281: administered a single 0.5 mg/kg oral dose of [^{14}C] mirdametinib and metabolism was primarily through oxidation to PD-0315209. Mirdametinib and PD-0315209 accounted for 97% to 100% of circulating radioactivity.
L282: The major plasma circulating drug-derived components in rat and dog consisted of mirdametinib and PD-0315209, thus the metabolism of [^{14}C] mirdametinib was extensive and similar across species.
L283: ## **Excretion**
L284: *Determination of Radiolabeled Routes of Excretion of [^{14}C] PD-325901 in Sprague-Dawley Rat (PDMK2005-0012PR)*
L285: *Determination of Radiolabeled Mass Balance and Routes of Excretion Of [^{14}C] PD-325901 in Beagle Dogs (PDMK2005-030PR)*
L286: **Excretion Routes:** Following oral administration of [^{14}C] mirdametinib to intact rats (PDMK2005-0012PR), the primary route of excretion was via the feces (\ge 82%), with approximately 11% of the radioactivity recovered in the urine. In bile-duct cannulated rats, the total recovery in the bile was 85% and 70% for males and females, respectively. In intact dogs, approximately 95% and 89% were recovered in the feces of male and female dogs, respectively, whereas the amount recovered in urine accounted for approximately 2% for both male and female dogs (PDMK2005-030PR).
L287: ## **Toxicokinetic data from general toxicology studies**
L288: *A GLP 5-Day and 28-Day Range-Finding Study of PD-0325901 by Oral Gavage in CByB6F1 Hybrid Mice (20196649)*
L289: *1-Month Oral Toxicity Study of PD-325901 in Rats (745-03826)*
L290: *3-Month Oral Toxicity Study of PD-325901 in Rats (05067)*
L291: *1-Month Oral Toxicity Study of PD-325901 in Dogs (745-03833)*
L292: *3-Month Oral Toxicity Study of PD-325901 in Dogs (05039)*
L293: In a 28-day study, mice were administered 10, 30, or 100 mg/kg PO mirdametinib QD (20196649). Mirdametinib maximum concentration (Cmax) and AUC from dosing to last quantifiable concentration (AUClast) for were higher in females than males on Day 1 and Day 28. Tmax was observed within 1 hour and increases in Cmax and AUClast on Day 1 were generally greater than dose proportional. Mirdametinib exposures were similar on Day 1 and Day 28, suggesting no accumulation. Exposure to PD-0315209 was similar in both sexes, with metabolite representing 20-43% of parent exposure in males and 15-27% in females. Accumulation of PD-0315209 was not observed. AUC0-24 for mirdametinib and PD-0315209 at the lowest observed adverse effect level (LOAEL) (10 mg/kg) and their safety margins relative to human exposure are provided in Table 4.
L294: In a 4-week study, rats were administered 0.1, 0.3, or 1 mg/kg PO mirdametinib QD (745-03826). Mirdametinib Cmax and area under the plasma concentration-time curve over the last 24-h dosing interval (AUC0-24) for were similar between females and males on Day 1 and Day 22 and mirdametinib plasma AUC0-24 increased in a dose proportional manner. Mirdametinib exposures were similar on Day 1 and Day 22, suggesting no accumulation. Exposure to PD-0315209 was greater in females than in males, with metabolite representing 27-50% of parent exposure in males and 134-174% in females. Accumulation of PD-0315209 was not observed. AUC0-24 for mirdametinib and PD-0315209 at the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) (0.1 mg/kg) and their safety margins relative to human exposure are provided in Table 4.
L295: In a 13-week study, rats were administered 0.1, 0.3, or 1 mg/kg PO mirdametinib QD (05067). Mirdametinib Cmax and AUClast for were similar between females and males on Day 1 and Day 89 and mirdametinib plasma AUC0-24 increased in a dose-related manner. Tmax was observed within 3.25 hours for males and 1.75 hours for females. Mirdametinib exposures were similar on Day 1 and Day 89, suggesting no accumulation. Exposure to PD-0315209 was higher in females than in males. Accumulation of PD-0315209 was not observed. AUC0-24 for mirdametinib and PD-0315209 at the NOAEL (0.1 mg/kg) and their safety margins relative to human exposure are provided in Table 4.
L296: In a 4-week study, dogs were administered 0.05, 0.1, or 0.3 mg/kg PO mirdametinib QD (745-03833). Mirdametinib Cmax and AUClast for were similar between females than males on Day 1 and Day 28 and increased in a dose-dependent manner. Exposure to PD-0315209 was similar in both sexes, with metabolite representing 23-34% of parent exposure in males and 22-35% in females. Accumulation of mirdametinib and PD-0315209 was not observed. AUC0-24 for mirdametinib and PD-0315209 at the NOAEL (0.1 mg/kg) and their safety margins relative to human exposure are provided in Table 4.
L297: In a 13-week study, dogs were administered 0.05, 0.1, or 0.4 mg/kg PO mirdametinib QD (05039). PK at Day 90 for the high dose was not obtained due to mortality. Mirdametinib Cmax and AUClast for were similar between females than males on Day 1, Day 41 and Day 90 and increased in a dose-dependent manner. Tmax was observed within 1.67 hours. Exposure to PD-0315209 was similar in both sexes and accumulation was not observed for mirdametinib or PD-0315209. AUC0-24 for mirdametinib and PD-0315209 at the NOAEL (0.1 mg/kg) and their safety margins relative to human exposure are provided in Table 4.
L298: ## Toxicokinetic data from carcinogenicity studies
L299: A 26-week Carcinogenicity Study of PD-0325901 by Oral Gavage in CByB6F1/Tg ras H2 Hemizygous Mice (20196651)
L300: In a 26-week study, mice were administered 2, 5, or 15 mg/kg PO mirdametinib QD (20196651). PK at Day 180 for the high dose was not obtained due to early termination of
L301: the group. Mirdametinib Cmax and AUClast were higher in females than males on Day 1 and Day 180. Tmax was observed within 1 hour and increases in Cmax and AUClast were slightly less than dose proportional. Mirdametinib exposures were similar on Day 1 and Day 180, suggesting minimal or no accumulation. Exposure to PD-0315209 was generally greater in females than in males. Overall, AUClast for PD-0315209 was 12.4-32.1% of what was observed for mirdametinib. Accumulation of PD-0315209 was not observed. AUC0-24 for mirdametinib and PD-0315209 at the maximum tolerated dose (MTD) (5 mg/kg) and their safety margins relative to human exposure are provided in Table 4.
L302: ## **Toxicokinetic data from reproductive toxicology studies**
L303: ## *Oral Dose Range-Finding Study of PD 0325901 in Pregnant Rabbits (2005-0052)*
L304: In an embryo-fetal toxicity study, pregnant female rabbits were administered 0.3, 1, 3, or 6 mg/kg PO mirdametinib QD during gestation days 7 through 19 (2005-0052). PK at the 3 and 6 mg/kg dose levels were not obtained due severe toxicity. Mirdametinib exposure increased in a generally dose proportional manner. Tmax was observed within 2.7 hours. The metabolite (PD-0315209) to parent ratio was 0.8 and 0.6 at the 0.3 and 1 mg/kg dose levels, respectively. AUC0-24 for mirdametinib and PD-0315209 at the LOAEL (0.3 mg/kg) and their safety margins relative to human exposure are provided in Table 4.

L306: Mirdametinib is rapidly absorbed, with Tmax typically being observed within 1 to 2 hours of oral administration. Exposure increases with dose in a generally dose proportional manner and minimal accumulation is observed following repeat dosing for either mirdametinib or its primary metabolite, PD-0315209. Absolute bioavailability of mirdametinib ranged from moderate (rat and monkey) to high (dog). Mirdametinib showed high protein binding in all evaluated species and was also found to be widely distributed into tissues, including exposure in the brain. Mirdametinib is extensively metabolized and primarily undergoes glucuronidation and oxidation of the parent compound. UGT1A6 and UGT2B7 are heavily involved in the metabolism of mirdametinib and its most abundant metabolite, PD-0315209, is produced by carboxylesterase variants CES1b and CES1c. The majority of the drug was excreted in the feces
L307: as metabolites, with only small amounts of unchanged mirdametinib found in urine or feces.
L308: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L309: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's assessment of the nonclinical ADME/PK data. FDA notes that following a single oral dose of 10 mg/kg, [^{14}C]-mirdametinib-derived radioactivity distributed widely to rat tissues, with greatest uptake in the liver, followed by kidney, blood, adrenal, lung, salivary gland, heart, and pancreas.
L310: Because the FDA calculated animal to human exposures based on their own analysis of nonclinical and clinical PK data, the FDA did not verify the results presented by the Applicant for Table 4 titled Calculated Safety Margins from Definitive Repeat-Dose Toxicity Studies.

L332: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L333: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L334: ## The Applicant's Position:
L335: Mirdametinib was assessed in a series of nonclinical toxicology studies in alignment with applicable International Conference on Harmonization (ICH) guidance documents. The oral route of exposure was selected for these studies as the oral route is the intended clinical route of administration. The rat and dog were selected as the general toxicology species based on mirdametinib PK and metabolism properties compared across species. The definitive studies were performed in the US in accordance with the US Department of Health and Human Services, FDA, US Code of Federal Regulations, Title 21, Part 58: GLP for Nonclinical Laboratory Studies.
L336: Exploratory studies were conducted in mice, rats, dogs, and monkeys, while definitive toxicity studies were conducted in rats and dogs. Studies were based on daily oral (gavage) dosing with a limited number of studies involving IV administration. Mirdametinib was administered to rats and dogs in repeat-dose general toxicology studies up to 3 months. The 1-month studies in rats and dogs were followed by a 1-month recovery period. A 1-month mouse study was conducted to support a 6-month carcinogenicity study in transgenic mice. A 2-year rat carcinogenicity study will be conducted as a postmarking commitment. Preliminary embryo-fetal development (EFD) toxicity studies were conducted in rats and rabbits, and a definitive fertility study was conducted in male and female rats. Juvenile animal studies were not conducted.

L363: ## NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
## Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
L364: Hematology	A moderate increase in leucocytes, fibrinogen and globulin combined with a decrease in albumin were suggestive of an inflammatory process and were correlated with macroscopic findings and microscopic findings of minimal multifocal neutrophilic inflammation of the mucosa of the tongue and esophagus in the 0.4 mg/kg/day group.
